❯ Give me a flow of how a query pair is tested for equivalence

  Searched for 1 pattern, listed 1 directory, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Equivalence is decided without running anything. Each query is parsed and
  rewritten into a canonical string, and the two strings are compared. The
  verdict comes from the first stage that can decide it.

  The flow

  pairs.jsonl line ──► cli.score_pair ──► equivalence.compare ──► Verdict(match,
  score, reason)

  0. Load and dispatch: cli.py (sqleval/cli.py)

  - load_schemas reads every <schema_id>.sql DDL file into a dict.
  - _read_pairs reads the JSONL and skips malformed lines.
  - score_pair (sqleval/cli.py#L28) checks that predicted and ground_truth are
    non-empty. If not, it returns no_prediction or no_ground_truth. It then
    calls compare(gt, pred, schema_ddl), and any exception becomes error:<Type>,
    so one bad pair can't stop the run.
  - With 400 or more pairs and more than one CPU, pairs are scored in a
    multiprocessing pool.

  1. Syntax gate on the prediction: validate.py (sqleval/validate.py)

  - parse_tree(predicted) runs PostgreSQL's real parser (libpg_query through
    pglast).
  - It fails with invalid:* (score 0) if the text is empty, has a syntax error,
    has more than one statement, or isn't a SELECT. SELECT includes VALUES, WITH
    and set operations.
  - _normalize removes location fields and rewrites NOT (x IS NULL) as x IS NOT
    NULL.

  2. Ground-truth analysis, cached: _analyze_gt (sqleval/equivalence.py#L29)

  - Thanks to lru_cache, the same ground truth is analysed only once, however
    many predictions it is compared with.
  - It produces three things: the pg parse tree, the canonical string, and
    whether the query has an outer ORDER BY.
  - If the ground truth won't parse, the verdict is ground_truth_unparseable.

  3. Fast path: identical parse trees

  - If pred_ast == gt_ast, the verdict is identical_parse_tree (match). This
    covers differences in whitespace, keyword case and the case of unquoted
    identifiers.

  4. Parse and resolve with sqlglot: parse_and_qualify
  (sqleval/canonical.py#L278)

  - The Context comes from the schema DDL, built by schema.py
    (sqleval/schema.py). It holds tables, column types, NOT NULL flags, PKs,
    UNIQUE constraints, FKs and enum labels, and it is cached per schema.
  - sqlglot parses the query as Postgres, normalizes identifiers, checks that
    the tables exist and runs qualify, so every column is bound to its table
    alias. It then checks GROUP BY validity.
  - If a column can't be resolved:
    - When Postgres also sees no such column, the verdict is
      invalid:unresolved_column.
    - When Postgres does see the columns, sqlglot has mis-parsed the query, and
      the verdict is Unsupported.
  - Unsupported (from either query) gives no match, with partial credit for text
    similarity (see step 7).

  5. Canonicalize: canonicalize_tree (sqleval/canonical.py#L3315)

  Both queries go through the same rewrite pipeline. The ground truth's
  outer-ORDER-BY flag is passed for both, so ordering only counts when the
  ground truth asks for it. Roughly in order:

  1. Structural cleanup. Strip parentheses, normalize grouping sets and clauses,
     drop LIMIT/OFFSET that do nothing, remove ORDER BYs that can't matter,
     inline deterministic CTEs.
  2. Set operations. Turn UNION into UNION ALL when the rows provably can't
     overlap. Turn recursive tree-walk CTEs and grand-total UNIONs into grouping
     sets.
  3. Alias hygiene. Make aliases unique, resolve ORDER BY aliases, prune unused
     columns of derived tables.
  4. Global rewrites (schema-aware through Resolver). EXCEPT on a key becomes
     NOT EXISTS. Grouped derived tables are merged. ROW_NUMBER() = 1 becomes
     DISTINCT ON. Value sets become semi-joins.
  5. Expression normalization (_norm_expr). Remove double negation, flip NOT
     over comparisons, expand BETWEEN into >= AND <=, turn single-item IN into
     =, turn = ANY into IN, shift typed literal bounds, and so on.
  6. Per-scope rewrites, two passes. These run innermost SELECT first
     (_scope_rewrites (sqleval/canonical.py#L1529)) and use keys and FKs from
     the catalog:
     - RIGHT JOIN becomes LEFT JOIN.
     - Scalar subqueries become joins, and correlated aggregates are
       decorrelated.
     - IN becomes EXISTS, and a LEFT JOIN anti-join becomes NOT EXISTS.
     - Redundant FK joins and self-joins on a key are removed.
     - DISTINCT, GROUP BY columns and NOT NULL checks are dropped when they are
       redundant, and COUNT(col) becomes COUNT(*) when the column is non-null.
     - Equality equivalence classes are computed.
  7. Final pass. Canonicalize counts, convert aggregates to scalar subqueries,
     clean up EXISTS, strip orders again.

  6. Serialize: serialize (sqleval/canonical.py#L3280)

  - The tree becomes a string in which order doesn't matter wherever SQL
    semantics don't care about it:
    - AND/OR terms, FROM relations and GROUP BY keys are sorted sets.
    - The operands of =, <>, + and * are sorted.
    - Equalities are grouped into EQC{...} classes.
  - Aliases are renamed to T:<table>#i. For self-joins, every alias permutation
    is tried (up to 720) and the lexicographically smallest string wins, so the
    choice of alias names doesn't matter.

  7. Decide and score: equivalence.py (sqleval/equivalence.py#L48)

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┬───────┬──────────────────────┐
  │                 Outcome                  │ match │        score         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ identical_parse_tree / canonical_match   │ ✅    │ 1.0                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────────────────┤
  │                                          │       │ 0.95 × Jaccard of    │
  │ not_equivalent                           │ ❌    │ tokens in the        │
  │                                          │       │ canonical strings    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────────────────┤
  │                                          │       │ 0.5 × Jaccard of     │
  │ unsupported / ground_truth_unsupported   │ ❌    │ tokens in the raw    │
  │                                          │       │ SQL                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ invalid:*, ground_truth_unparseable,     │ ❌    │ 0.0                  │
  │ no_prediction, no_ground_truth, error:*  │       │                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┴───────┴──────────────────────┘

  8. Report: _report (sqleval/cli.py#L106)

  - {id, match, score} is written per pair.
  - It prints counts per verdict reason and the match rate per schema.
  - If pairs carry an equivalent label, it also prints a confusion matrix,
    precision, recall and F1, with accuracy broken down by source and category.

  Things to keep in mind

  - A match is only a proof one way. A canonical match means the rewrites showed
    the queries are equivalent. A not_equivalent verdict only means the
    rewrites couldn't line them up; the queries may still be equivalent (a false
    negative).
  - Without a schema, fewer rewrites fire. An unknown schema_id means none of
    the key, FK or NOT NULL rewrites can run, so queries that are equivalent are
    more likely to be scored as non-matches.

❯ How do the hand generated test vs auto vs malformed ones differ?

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Before writing it up, I'll run the evaluator to see how each source actually
  scores.

  Listed 1 directory, ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ All three come out of build_pairs.py (generator/build_pairs.py). For each of
  the 181 target queries in queries.jsonl (dataset/queries.jsonl), it emits hand
  pairs first, then auto pairs, then one malformed pair. They differ in who
  writes the prediction, how its label is known, and what each set is meant to
  test.

  At a glance

  Written by
  hand (635): A person, in generator/pairs/&lt;schema&gt;.py
  (generator/pairs/library.py)
  auto (1,230): Rule-based rewrites of the ground truth, in mutations.py
  (generator/mutations.py)
  malformed (181): Fixed templates in malformed() (generator/mutations.py#L469)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Labels
  hand (635): 248 equivalent, 387 not
  auto (1,230): 721 equivalent, 509 not
  malformed (181): All 181 not equivalent
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where the label comes from
  hand (635): The author's judgment, plus a note giving the reason
  auto (1,230): The rewrite rule: each rule is either known to preserve meaning
  or known to break it
  malformed (181): By definition: none of these is exactly one valid SELECT
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  category
  hand (635): semantic, or invalid_sql (14 pairs)
  auto (1,230): The name of the rewrite rule (rename_aliases, flip_comparison,
  …)
  malformed (181): The kind of garbage (prose, null, …)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Per query
  hand (635): 0–N, as many as the author wrote
  auto (1,230): Up to 4 equivalent and up to 4 not
  malformed (181): Exactly 1
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What it tests
  hand (635): Real equivalence reasoning
  auto (1,230): The basics: form doesn't matter, any change in meaning is caught
  malformed (181): The syntax gate

  Hand: the hard cases

  These are tuples of (E|N, sql, note), written to look like plausible model
  output. They differ from the ground truth in ways that need real
  understanding:
  - Equivalent, but structured differently. A correlated COUNT(*) > 1 instead of
    GROUP BY … HAVING. IN with DISTINCT instead of a join. '2024-06-01'::date
    instead of DATE '…'. Lowercased camelCase identifiers, which Postgres folds
    to the same names.
  - Not equivalent, with subtle bugs. Grouping by title merges books that share
    a title. Dropping DISTINCT lists two authors with the same name twice. <=
    instead of < on a date. LIMIT with no ORDER BY. 'ON' vs 'on'. Picking the
    wrong table's name column.
  - INVALID: notes. For example, a quoted "bookAuthors", which is
    case-sensitive, so it doesn't match the table bookauthors.

  When the dataset is built, it checks that:
  - every equivalent hand prediction parses and resolves against the schema;
  - a non-equivalent one is also valid, unless its note starts with INVALID:, in
    which case it must fail;
  - no non-equivalent prediction has the same parse tree as the target.

  Auto: wide but shallow

  Every rewrite is applied to the ground truth itself, so it is only ever one
  edit away from it.
  - Equivalent rewrites come in two kinds:
    - Cosmetic (trailing_semicolon, leading_comment, lowercase, reformatting,
      <>/!=). These are checked to leave the Postgres parse tree unchanged, and
      at most one is used per query.
    - Structural (rename_aliases, swap_conjuncts_and_join_sides, implicit_joins,
      count_one, add_order_by_unordered_target). These are sound by
      construction.
  - Breaking rewrites also come in two kinds:
    - Semantic (flip_comparison, change_literal, drop_filter, swap_aggregate,
      left_to_inner_join, flip_null_check, drop_distinct, ordering and limit
      changes, …).
    - Structural, at most one per query (swap_columns, drop_column, plus
      quote_identifiers and unquote_identifiers, which are expected to produce
      invalid SQL).
    - The rules only apply where the change can't accidentally keep the meaning.
      For example, comparisons are only flipped against literals. A breaking
      rewrite must change the parse tree, and except for the quoting ones it
      must still be valid SQL.
  - Which rules get used is random, but seeded from each query's id, so
    rebuilding gives the same pairs.

  Malformed: never a match

  For each query, the builder takes one kind from a fixed list, moving one step
  along the list per query: null, empty, markdown_fenced, two_statements, prose,
  truncated, mysql_backticks, dml_statement (DELETE FROM …), misspelled_keyword
  (SELEC). These test the pglast gate and the no_prediction path in the
  evaluator. They never reach canonicalization.

  How the evaluator does on each

  I ran ./score on the current dataset:

  hand        631/635   99.4%    ← all 4 misses are equivalent pairs scored as
  non-matches (false negatives)
  auto       1230/1230 100.0%
  malformed   181/181  100.0%
  overall: precision 100%, recall 99.6%, 0 false positives

  Hand is the only set that really stresses the evaluator, and it is where all
  the errors are. Auto and malformed mostly guard against regressions: they make
  sure surface changes still match, single-edit bugs are caught and garbage is
  rejected.

⏺ The new cases turned up 3 false positives, which means the evaluator called

  Read 1 file, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I added 90 hand cases to campus and sales_star (635 → 725 hand pairs). They
  exposed 3 false positives, where the evaluator calls non-equivalent queries a
  match; before this it had none. The pairs rebuild cleanly and the unit tests
  in tests/test_equivalence.py pass.

  Coverage we were missing

  I checked two things: which SQL features appear in the 181 targets and the
  hand predictions, and which canonicalization rules the hand pairs actually
  trigger.

  1. PostgreSQL syntax no hand prediction used, though a model could produce any
  of these: LATERAL, FULL/RIGHT/CROSS JOIN, = ANY (ARRAY[...]), <> ALL, row
  comparison (a, b) = (x, y), FETCH … WITH TIES, ROLLUP and GROUPING SETS, named
  WINDOW, LAG with a default, NULLS FIRST/LAST, date - INTERVAL, and IS NOT
  DISTINCT FROM. Seven of these 11 had zero hand predictions; ROLLUP, GROUPING
  SETS, IS NOT DISTINCT FROM and the other FETCH forms appeared only once or
  twice.

  2. Thinly tested rules. About 10 canonicalization rules fired on only 1–3
  pairs, so the cases where their preconditions fail were never tested.
  Examples: _right_to_left, _split_in_intersect, _default_null_ordering,
  _select_star_of_setop, _normalize_clauses.

  3. Semantic edge cases that were missing:
  - Empty tables, where one query returns NULL and the other raises division by
    zero.
  - Values that a CHECK constraint guarantees (is_active <> 0 is the same as =
    1).
  - Two date columns that nothing in the schema ties together (year vs
    full_date).
  - Functions from other SQL dialects, such as IFNULL.
  - Type errors (smallint = TRUE).

  4. Target-side gaps. There are no targets with window frames, DISTINCT ON,
  LATERAL, CASE or string functions; window functions appear in only 4 targets
  and DISTINCT ON in 2. Hand cases can only vary predictions, so closing this
  means adding new queries to queries.jsonl.

  Results with the new cases

  hand   696/725  96.0%   (was 99.4%)    precision 99.7%   recall 97.4%
  FP 3 (was 0)   FN 26 (was 4)

  The false positives are real evaluator bugs:
  1. FETCH FIRST n ROWS WITH TIES is treated as LIMIT n. canonical.py:809-811
     (sqleval/canonical.py#L809-L811) rewrites the FETCH into a LIMIT and drops
     with_ties. This accounts for 2 of the 3 false positives (campus_007 and
     sales_star_003).
  2. IFNULL(...) matches COALESCE. sqlglot quietly turns IFNULL into Coalesce,
     and the pglast syntax check only checks grammar, not whether a function
     exists. So a query that PostgreSQL would reject is scored as a match
     (campus_019).

  The 22 new false negatives are equivalences the rewrites can't line up yet.
  They fall into a few groups:
  - Constraint facts: CHECK constraints, and NOT NULL on aggregate results,
    which NULLS LAST depends on.
  - Restructured queries: LATERAL, ROW_NUMBER filtering instead of LIMIT, a
    window AVG instead of a scalar subquery, a correlated COUNT = 0, and HAVING
    COUNT(col) = 0 over a LEFT JOIN.
  - Small normalizations: = ANY(ARRAY), row comparisons, named windows, LAG(x,
    1), 1e4, GROUP BY as a replacement for DISTINCT, and a pass-through derived
    table.

  Validator gaps (no wrong scores, but invalid SQL passes as valid):
  - Bad GROUP BY. The validator doesn't catch PostgreSQL's "must appear in GROUP
    BY" error. My INVALID: case for it failed the build, so it is now an
    ordinary non-equivalent case.
  - Type errors. WHERE is_active = TRUE passes the gate, though PostgreSQL
    rejects it. It still scores as non-equivalent, but for the wrong reason.

  Next steps

  1. Fix the two false positives. Make _normalize_clauses keep WITH TIES (or
     treat such queries as unsupported), and reject functions PostgreSQL doesn't
     have, such as IFNULL, NVL and ISNULL(a, b).
  2. Extend the cases to the other 6 schemas. library, org_chart,
     project_tracker, sales_snowflake, social_graph and web_analytics have the
     same gaps, plus quoted, camelCase and ENUM identifier behaviour that campus
     and sales_star don't exercise.
  3. Add targets with window frames, DISTINCT ON, LATERAL and CASE, so the hand
     cases can test those features as the ground truth too.

  Which of these should I do first? I'd start with the two false-positive fixes,
  since a false match is worse than a missed one.

  new labels depend on CHECK constraints, I need to revisit them before
  continuing.

      377 +- **One-sided  reported as non-matches. The 96 false negatives
          + fall into these groups:                                      
      378 +  - `LATERAL` lookups, top-N and aggregates, and `ROW_NUMBER`/
          +`RANK` filters in place of                                    
      379 +    `LIMIT` or `DISTINCT ON` (about 20);                      
      380 +  - facts the rewrites don't derive: integer bounds on counts 
          +(`> 2` vs `>= 3`), aggregates that                            
      381 +    are never NULL (so `NULLS LAST` is a no-op), enum declarat
          +ion order, `AVG` as                                           
      382 +    `SUM / COUNT`, `RANK` = `ROW_NUMBER` under a unique orderi
          +ng;                                                           
      383 +  - surface forms that are not normalized: named `WINDOW`s, `L
          +AG(x, 1)`, `= ANY (ARRAY[...])`,                              
      384 +    row-value comparisons, `GROUP BY` in place of `DISTINCT`, 
          +`CASE` branch order, `1e4`,                                   
      385 +    date `+ INTERVAL`, `unnest`/`LATERAL VALUES` unpivots, `RI
          +GHT JOIN` before a further join;                              
      386 +  - recursive CTEs seeded differently, and correlated counts o
          +r sums in place of joins;                                     
      387    - distributing a division (`Q*P*(1 - C/100)` vs. `Q*P*C/100`
           ): NUMERIC division rounds to a
      381 -    scale that depends on the dividend's magnitude, so the two
          - can differ for very large values;                            
      382 -  - an average of per-post counts vs. a ratio of table counts:
          - needs the counts to be summed                                
      383 -    through a foreign key, and the naive `COUNT/COUNT` form di
          -vides by zero where `AVG` is NULL.                            
      388 +    scale that depends on the dividend's magnitude, so the two
          + can differ for very large values.                            
      389 +foreign key, and the naive `COUNT/COUNT` form divides by zero 
          +where `AVG` is NULL.                                          
      390  - **CHECK constraints are ignored.** The task defines validity
            by types, keys, NOT NULL and
      391    foreign keys, so `flag <> 0` is not treated as equal to `fla
           g = 1`. Enum types are used.
      392  - **Numeric display scale is not modelled.** Results are compa
           red by value semantics. Rewrites

⏺ I did all three next steps. The dataset now has 197 targets and 2,530 pairs,
  and the evaluator has no false positives. Both builds pass and the unit tests
  pass. Nothing is committed.

  1. The false-positive fixes

  - FETCH … WITH TIES is no longer treated as LIMIT n (canonical.py:809
    (sqleval/canonical.py#L809)). While fixing it I found a second bug in the
    same place: FETCH FIRST ROW ONLY has no explicit count, so its limit was
    dropped completely and a one-row query matched an unlimited one. It now
    means LIMIT 1.
  - Functions from other dialects are rejected, such as IFNULL, NVL,
    two-argument ISNULL, DATEDIFF and YEAR() (validate.py
    (sqleval/validate.py)). The check runs in the pglast gate, which only checks
    grammar, so these calls used to get through. sqlglot then quietly turned
    some of them into COALESCE.
  - The generator's validity check (build_queries.py
    (generator/build_queries.py)) now runs the same gate, so cases using these
    functions can be labelled INVALID:.
  - I added 7 unit tests covering these cases.

  2. Hand cases for the other six schemas

  I added 205 cases across library, org_chart, project_tracker, sales_snowflake,
  social_graph and web_analytics. They cover the same gaps as before, plus a
  few specific to these schemas:
  - enum declaration order ('yes' < 'no', status < 'done');
  - WITH TIES when the last sort key is unique, where it matches LIMIT;
  - EXCEPT against a nullable column, which isn't affected by NULLs the way NOT
    IN is;
  - a ::char(2) cast that truncates the value;
  - unnest and LATERAL VALUES used to unpivot rows.

  3. New targets

  16 new targets, two per schema, cover CASE buckets, DISTINCT ON, LATERAL
  top-N, window frames (ROWS vs RANGE INTERVAL, centred, running total), LAG
  partitions and split_part/length. Each has 3–5 hand cases, and the build adds
  their auto and malformed pairs. The new tags are lateral, window_frame and
  string_func.

  Labels I changed to fit the project's rules

  - CHECK constraints don't count. session/session1.txt records that equivalence
    uses only types, keys, NOT NULL, foreign keys and enum types. I had
    labelled 9 cases equivalent because of a CHECK constraint (is_active <> 0 vs
    = 1), so I relabelled them as not equivalent and added a note saying why.
  - I removed 2 "distributed division" cases, because the README notes NUMERIC
    division can round differently for very large values.
  - My relabel script had a bug, and I fixed it. Its regex matched across
    several entries and wrongly flipped 9 existing equivalent pairs to not
    equivalent. The build caught it. I restored those 9, then checked all 354
    cases I added against my source data, with no mismatches.

  Results

  pairs 2,530   accuracy 96.2%   precision 100% (FP 0)   recall 92.0% (FN 96)
  hand 892/988 (90.3%)   auto 1345/1345   malformed 197/197
  The 96 false negatives are all hand-written equivalences the rewrites can't
  line up yet. The largest groups are:
  - LATERAL and ROW_NUMBER forms;
  - facts the rewrites don't derive, such as integer bounds on counts and
    aggregates that can never be NULL;
  - forms that aren't normalized yet, such as named windows, = ANY (ARRAY…) and
    row-value comparisons.

  I updated the README to match: counts, tags, the validity gate, the pair
  themes, the results and the known limitations.

  A correction to my last message: the evaluator does catch the "column must
  appear in GROUP BY" error. It's only the generator's validity check that
  misses it, which is why that one case is a plain non-equivalent pair rather
  than INVALID:.

  The biggest remaining gain in recall would be a rewrite that treats LATERAL …
  LIMIT 1 and ROW_NUMBER() = 1 as the same as a join or DISTINCT ON; it would
  cover about 20 of the false negatives.

  string. None failed to parse or was flagged as unsupported. Grouped by the
  missing rule, each pair counted once:

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 10
  Missing rule: Recursive CTEs seeded differently
  Count: 3
  Example: a scalar vs a joined seed; seeding with the root and filtering it out
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 11
  Missing rule: RIGHT JOIN followed by another join
  Count: 2
  Example: a lone RIGHT JOIN is matched; a chained one isn't
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 12
  Missing rule: WITH TIES with a unique last sort key
  Count: 1
  Example: conservative on purpose since my fix

  Group 1, value and expression forms (22):
  - CASE, 5 pairs: branches reordered, simple vs searched CASE, COALESCE(CASE
    …), SUM(CASE … 1 ELSE -1).
  - Date/timestamp + INTERVAL not constant-folded: 3.
  - String functions: 3 pairs (strpos/position vs ILIKE, char_length vs length).
  - = ANY (ARRAY[…]) vs IN/=: 2.
  - Row-value (a, b) = (x, y): 2.
  - One pair each: 1e4, a - b > 0, IS DISTINCT FROM on a NOT NULL column,
    COALESCE(x, 0) = 0 vs x IS NULL OR x = 0, LIKE '%', LEAST/GREATEST with
    DISTINCT.
  - The distributed-division pair: missed on purpose, since the README says it
    can round differently.

  Group 3, window functions (12):
  - 6 are a window function vs a different construct: ROW_NUMBER/RANK filters vs
    LIMIT or LATERAL top-N, a window AVG vs a scalar subquery, a RANGE INTERVAL
    frame vs a correlated sum.
  - 6 are syntax that isn't normalized: named WINDOW w (4), LAG(x) vs LAG(x, 1),
    and a default frame vs an explicit ROWS UNBOUNDED PRECEDING.

  Group 4, facts about aggregate results (16):
  - 6 are integer bounds on counts (COUNT(*) > 3 vs >= 4). These work on integer
    columns but not on COUNT.
  - 5 are aggregates that can never be NULL, so NULLS LAST/NULLS FIRST changes
    nothing. This works on NOT NULL columns but not on COUNT or SUM over a NOT
    NULL column.
  - 3 are AVG(x) vs SUM(x) / COUNT(*).
  - 2 are NULLIF(divisor, 0) where the target is already NULL.

  How I confirmed this: I ran 26 minimal probe pairs, one per suspected gap. The
  ones that should match only under the rule the dataset says is missing did
  miss. The ones the README says are handled did match: integer bounds on
  columns, NULLS LAST on NOT NULL columns, CASE vs COALESCE on its own, and a
  single RIGHT JOIN. So each group really is one missing rule, not something
  specific to the dataset.

  What would close the most:
  1. Treat LATERAL as a join. Lookups by key and top-1/top-N would cover about
     12 pairs, and up to 18 if ROW_NUMBER() <= n filters are mapped to the same
     form.
  2. Make the NULL and integer-bound facts work for aggregate results (11
     pairs). COUNT returns bigint and is never NULL, and SUM/AVG over a
     non-empty group of NOT NULL values are never NULL either.
  3. Normalize simple surface forms (about 12 pairs, all low-risk): named
     windows, LAG(x, 1), = ANY (ARRAY), row-value =, numeric literals, DISTINCT
     ↔ GROUP BY, and fold date + INTERVAL into a constant.
  4. Recognize LEFT JOIN … HAVING COUNT(col) = 0 and correlated COUNT(*) = 0 as
     anti-joins (4 pairs).

  Groups 10 and 12, and the distributed-division pair, are better left as
  misses. Matching them safely would take a lot of extra reasoning, or would
  mean matching cases that can really differ.

❯ Okay update it to account for all the groups but 10 and 12

⏺ I found a more serious issue: the serializer ignores the WINDOW clause, so
  queries differing only in named-window definitions incorrectly match (a latent
  false positive) — inlining named windows will fix this. Next I'll check how
  sqlglot's transform visits nodes it has already replaced.

      1085 +def _interval_value(e):                                      
      1086 +    """(months, days, seconds) of an INTERVAL '<n> <unit>' li
           +teral with an integer n, else None."""                       
      1087 +    if not isinstance(e, exp.Interval) or not isinstance(e.th
           +is, exp.Literal):                                            
      1088 +        return None                                          
      1089 +    text = e.this.this.strip()                               
      1090 +    unit = e.args.get("unit")                                
      1091 +    unit = unit.name if unit is not None else None           
      1092 +    if unit is None:                                         
      1093 +        m = re.fullmatch(r"(-?\d+)\s*([A-Za-z]+)", text)     
      1094 +        if not m:                                            
      1095 +            return None                                      
      1096 +        text, unit = m.group(1), m.group(2)                  
      1097 +    if not re.fullmatch(r"-?\d+", text):                     
      1098 +        return None                                          
      1099 +    u = unit.upper()                                         
      1100 +    u = u[:-1] if u.endswith("S") else u                     
      1101 +    if u not in _INTERVAL_UNITS:                             
      1102 +        return None                                          
      1103 +    field, mult = _INTERVAL_UNITS[u]                         
      1104 +    n = int(text) * mult                                     
      1105 +    return (n, 0, 0) if field == "m" else (0, n, 0) if field 
           +== "d" else (0, 0, n)

      1108 +def _add_interval(value, months, days, seconds):             
      1109 +    """PostgreSQL timestamp + interval: months first (clampin
           +g the day to the month's end), then                          
      1110 +    days, then the time part."""                             
      1111 +    import calendar                                          
      1112 +    y, m = divmod(value.month - 1 + months, 12)              
      1113 +    y, m = value.year + y, m + 1                             
      1114 +    if not 1 <= y <= 9999:                                   
      1115 +        raise ValueError("out of range")                     
      1116 +    day = min(value.day, calendar.monthrange(y, m)[1])       
      1117 +    out = value.replace(year=y, month=m, day=day)            
      1118 +    return out + _dt.timedelta(days=days, seconds=seconds)   
      1119 +                                                             
      1120 +                                                             
      1121 +def _fold_date_arithmetic(node):                             
      1122 +    """DATE / TIMESTAMP literal +/- INTERVAL literal -> TIMES
           +TAMP literal; DATE literal +/- integer                       
      1123 +    -> DATE literal (PostgreSQL's result types)."""          
      1124 +    if not isinstance(node, (exp.Add, exp.Sub)):             
      1125 +        return None                                          
      1126 +    sign = -1 if isinstance(node, exp.Sub) else 1            
      1127 +    for a, b in ((node.this, node.expression), (node.expressi
           +on, node.this)):                                             
      1128 +        if sign < 0 and a is not node.this:                  
      1129 +            break  # interval - date is not defined          
      1130 +        tl = _typed_literal(a)                               
      1131 +        if tl is None:                                       
      1132 +            continue                                         
      1133 +        kind, val = tl                                       
      1134 +        try:                                                 
      1135 +            iv = _interval_value(b)                          
      1136 +            if iv is not None:                               
      1137 +                start = _dt.datetime(val.year, val.month, val
           +.day) if kind == "DATE" else val                             
      1138 +                return _make_typed("TIMESTAMP", _add_interval
           +(start, *(sign * x for x in iv)))                            
      1139 +            n = _lit_int(b)                                  
      1140 +            if n is not None and kind == "DATE":             
      1141 +                return _make_typed("DATE", val + _dt.timedelt
           +a(days=sign * n))                                            
      1142 +        except (ValueError, OverflowError):                  
      1143 +            return None                                      
      1144 +    return None                                              
      1145 +                                                             
      1146 +                                                             
      1147 +def _num_literal(e):                                         
      1148 +    """A numeric literal in its canonical spelling (10000.0 a
           +nd 1e4 -> 10000), for comparisons."""                        
      1149 +    if not isinstance(e, exp.Literal) or e.is_string:        
      1150 +        return None                                          
      1151 +    try:                                                     
      1152 +        d = Decimal(e.this)                                  
      1153 +    except (InvalidOperation, ValueError):                   
      1154 +        return None                                          
      1155 +    if not d.is_finite():                                    
      1156 +        return None                                          
      1157 +    s = str(int(d)) if d == d.to_integral_value() else format
           +(d.normalize(), "f")                                         
      1158 +    return None if s == e.this else exp.Literal.number(s)    
      1159 +                                                             
      1160 +                                                             
      1161 +def _is_count(e):                                            
      1162 +    if isinstance(e, exp.Filter):                            
      1163 +        e = e.this                                           
      1164 +    return isinstance(e, exp.Count)                          
      1165 +                                                             
      1166 +                                                             
      1167 +def _count_subquery(e):                                      
      1168 +    """The SELECT of a scalar (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM ... WHERE
           + ...) without GROUP BY, or None."""                          
      1169 +    if not isinstance(e, exp.Subquery) or not isinstance(e.th
           +is, exp.Select):                                             
      1170 +        return None                                          
      1171 +    s = e.this                                               
      1172 +    if len(s.expressions) != 1 or any(s.args.get(k) for k in 
           +("group", "having", "limit", "offset", "with", "distinct")): 
      1173 +        return None                                          
      1174 +    c = _unalias(s.expressions[0])                           
      1175 +    if isinstance(c, exp.Count) and isinstance(c.this, (exp.S
           +tar, exp.Literal)) and not isinstance(c.parent, exp.Filter) \
      1176 +            and not s.find(exp.Window):                      
      1177 +        return s                                             
      1178 +    return None                                              
      1179 +                                                             
      1180 +                                                             
      1181 +def _like_matches(pattern, s, ci):                           
      1182 +    """PostgreSQL LIKE / ILIKE with the default escape charac
           +ter."""                                                      
      1183 +    rx, i = "", 0                                            
      1184 +    while i < len(pattern):                                  
      1185 +        ch = pattern[i]                                      
      1186 +        if ch == "\\" and i + 1 < len(pattern):              
      1187 +            rx += re.escape(pattern[i + 1])                  
      1188 +            i += 2                                           
      1189 +            continue                                         
      1190 +        rx += ".*" if ch == "%" else "." if ch == "_" else re
           +.escape(ch)                                                  
      1191 +        i += 1                                               
      1192 +    return re.fullmatch(rx, s, re.S | (re.I if ci else 0)) is
           + not None                                                    
      1193 +                                                             
      1194 +                                                             
      1195 +def _norm_expr_extra(node, R: Resolver):                     
      1196 +    """Exact identities added on top of _norm_expr's (each ho
           +lds for NULL inputs too)."""                                 
      1197 +    # --- date arithmetic on literals -----------------------
           +--------------------------------------                       
      1198 +    folded = _fold_date_arithmetic(node)                     
      1199 +    if folded is not None:                                   
      1200 +        return folded                                        
      1201 +    # --- x = ANY (ARRAY[...]) is x IN (...); x <> ALL (ARRAY
           +[...]) is x NOT IN (...) ----------------                    
      1202 +    if isinstance(node, (exp.EQ, exp.NEQ)) and isinstance(nod
           +e.expression, exp.Any if isinstance(node, exp.EQ) else exp.Al
           +l):                                                          
      1203 +        arr = node.expression.this                           
      1204 +        arr = arr.this if isinstance(arr, exp.Paren) else arr
      1205 +        if isinstance(arr, exp.Array) and arr.expressions:   
      1206 +            test = exp.In(this=node.this, expressions=[v.copy
           +() for v in arr.expressions])                                
      1207 +            return test if isinstance(node, exp.EQ) else exp.
           +Not(this=test)                                               
      1208 +    # --- row comparisons: (a, b) = (x, y) is a = x AND b = y
           +; <> is the OR of <> ---------------------                   
      1209 +    if isinstance(node, (exp.EQ, exp.NEQ)) and isinstance(nod
           +e.this, exp.Tuple) and isinstance(node.expression, exp.Tuple)
           + \                                                           
      1210 +            and len(node.this.expressions) == len(node.expres
           +sion.expressions) >= 1:                                      
      1211 +        parts = [type(node)(this=a.copy(), expression=b.copy(
           +))                                                           
      1212 +                 for a, b in zip(node.this.expressions, node.
           +expression.expressions)]                                     
      1213 +        out = parts[0]                                       
      1214 +        for p in parts[1:]:                                  
      1215 +            out = (exp.And if isinstance(node, exp.EQ) else e
           +xp.Or)(this=out, expression=p)                               
      1216 +        return out                                           
      1217 +    # --- IS [NOT] DISTINCT FROM between never-NULL operands 
           +is = / <> -------------------------------                    
      1218 +    if isinstance(node, (exp.NullSafeEQ, exp.NullSafeNEQ)) an
           +d not isinstance(node.expression, exp.Null) \                
      1219 +            and all(R.not_null(s) for s in (node.this, node.e
           +xpression)):                                                 
      1220 +        return (exp.EQ if isinstance(node, exp.NullSafeEQ) el
           +se exp.NEQ)(this=node.this, expression=node.expression)      
      1221 +    # --- COALESCE(x, c) = c is x IS NULL OR x = c ----------
           +----------------------------------------                     
      1222 +    if isinstance(node, exp.EQ):                             
      1223 +        for co, c in ((node.this, node.expression), (node.exp
           +ression, node.this)):

           +sions) == 1 and _literal_value(c) \

           +_literal_value(c) and co.expressions[0].is_string == c.is_str
           +ing:                                                         
      1226 +                x = co.this                                  
      1227 +                return exp.Or(this=exp.Is(this=x.copy(), expr
           +ession=exp.Null()),                                          
      1228 +                              expression=exp.EQ(this=x.copy()
           +, expression=c.copy()))                                      
      1229 +    # --- char_length is length -----------------------------
           +-------------------------------------                        
      1230 +    if isinstance(node, exp.Anonymous) and node.name.lower() 
           +in ("char_length", "character_length") \                     
      1231 +            and len(node.expressions) == 1:                  
      1232 +        return exp.Length(this=node.expressions[0].copy())   
      1233 +    # --- strpos(s, p) > 0 is s LIKE '%p%' (p without wildcar
           +ds) --------------------------------------                   
      1234 +    if isinstance(node, (exp.GT, exp.GTE, exp.LT, exp.LTE, ex
           +p.EQ, exp.NEQ)):                                             
      1235 +        for pos, k, flipped in ((node.this, node.expression,

      1240 +            else:

      1242 +            pat, v = _lit_str(p), _lit_int(k)

           +ch in "%_\\"):                                               
      1244 +                continue                                     
      1245 +            op = type(node)                                  
      1246 +            if flipped:  # k op pos  ->  pos op' k           
      1247 +                op = {exp.GT: exp.LT, exp.GTE: exp.LTE, exp.L
           +T: exp.GT, exp.LTE: exp.GTE}.get(op, op)                     
      1248 +            found = (op is exp.GT and v == 0) or (op is exp.G
           +TE and v == 1) or (op is exp.NEQ and v == 0)                 
      1249 +            missing = (op is exp.EQ and v == 0) or (op is exp
           +.LT and v == 1) or (op is exp.LTE and v == 0)                
      1250 +            if found or missing:                             
      1251 +                like = exp.Like(this=s.copy(), expression=exp
           +.Literal.string(f"%{pat}%"))                                 
      1252 +                return like if found else exp.Not(this=like) 
      1253 +    # --- simple CASE is a searched CASE of equalities ------
           +---------------------------------------                      
      1254 +    if isinstance(node, exp.Case) and node.this is not None: 
      1255 +        x = node.this

      1257 +               for i in node.args.get("ifs") or []]

      1261 +        return out                                           
      1262 +    # --- an enum cast to text, matched against a pattern: th
           +e labels that match --------------------

      1267 +        if info and info.enum_labels and not node.args.get("e
           +scape"):

      1269 +            hits = [l for l in info.enum_labels              
      1270 +                    if (l == pat if isinstance(node, exp.EQ) 
           +else _like_matches(pat, l, isinstance(node, exp.ILike)))]    
      1271 +            if hits:

           +teral.string(hits[0]))

           +ral.string(h) for h in hits])                                
      1276 +    # --- (SELECT COUNT(*) ...) = 0 is NOT EXISTS (...); > 0 
           +is EXISTS ------------------------------                     
      1277 +    if isinstance(node, (exp.EQ, exp.NEQ, exp.LT, exp.LTE, ex
           +p.GT, exp.GTE)):

           +lse), (node.expression, node.this, True)):

      1282 +                continue                                     
      1283 +            op = type(node)                                  
      1284 +            if flipped:                                      
      1285 +                op = {exp.GT: exp.LT, exp.GTE: exp.LTE, exp.L
           +T: exp.GT, exp.LTE: exp.GTE}.get(op, op)                     
      1286 +            some = (op is exp.GT and v == 0) or (op is exp.GT
           +E and v == 1) or (op is exp.NEQ and v == 0)                  
      1287 +            none = (op is exp.EQ and v == 0) or (op is exp.LT
           + and v == 1) or (op is exp.LTE and v == 0)                   
      1288 +            if some or none:                                 
      1289 +                sub = s.copy()                               
      1290 +                sub.set("expressions", [exp.Literal.number(1)
           +])                                                           
      1291 +                ex = exp.Exists(this=sub)                    
      1292 +                return ex if some else exp.Not(this=ex)      
      1293 +    # --- a - b compared with 0 is a compared with b (exact N
           +UMERIC, or counts: no overflow) ---------                    
      1294 +    if isinstance(node, (exp.EQ, exp.NEQ, exp.LT, exp.LTE, ex
           +p.GT, exp.GTE)):

           +, (node.expression, node.this, False)):

           +d not isinstance(z, exp.Neg) and (                           
      1297 +                    _exact_kind(d, R) == "numeric" or (_is_co
           +unt(d.this) and _is_count(d.expression))):                   
      1298 +                a, b = d.this.copy(), d.expression.copy()    
      1299 +                return type(node)(this=a, expression=b) if le
           +ft else type(node)(this=b, expression=a)                     
      1300 +    # --- numeric literals compared in canonical spelling ---
           +-------------------------------------------

           +p.GT, exp.GTE)):

      1310 +    return node

⏺ Before wiring in the new expression rules, I'm fixing an unsound case:

  literals, which breaks text values like 'a ', so it now requires an exact
  match. I'm also updating _exact_kind to handle aggregates and making the
  simple-CASE rewrite skip volatile operands.

⏺ I traced the new false negatives and test failures to the COUNT bounds
  rewrite, which produces 2 <= COUNT(*) while existing rules expect the strict
  form 1 < COUNT(*). I'll fix this by normalizing aggregates the opposite way:
  strict lower bounds and inclusive upper bounds.

  ⎿  Updated README.md (+89 -25)
      242       `GROUP BY`/`ORDER BY`, `*` expansion, `USING` → `ON`. CTE
           s become derived tables (when read
      243       more than once, only if deterministic: no `random()`, `ne
           xtval`, ...). Aliases are made
      244       globally unique. Derived-table columns nothing reads are 
           dropped (not under `DISTINCT`), and
      245 -     the rest are renamed by position.                        
      245 +     the rest are sorted and renamed by position (only their n
          +ames are read). `OVER w` is replaced                          
      246 +     by the `WINDOW w` definition (an unresolved name is not c
          +anonicalized).                                                
      247 +   - *LATERAL, derived tables and unpivots:*                  
      248 +     - `JOIN LATERAL (q) x`: a plain `q` becomes a join with q
          +'s tables and WHERE; a `LEFT JOIN                             
      249 +       LATERAL` over one table whose outputs are its columns b
          +ecomes `LEFT JOIN T ON …`; an                                 
      250 +       ungrouped aggregate `q` (exactly one row) becomes scala
          +r subqueries; `q … ORDER BY o LIMIT 1`                        
      251 +       from a single keyed outer table becomes `DISTINCT ON (<
          +its key>) … ORDER BY <key>, o`;                               
      252 +       `q … LIMIT n` over one table correlated by one equality
          + `T.f = <outer column>` becomes a                             
      253 +       join to `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY T.f ORDER BY o
          +) <= n`; and the previous (next) row by a                     
      254 +       NOT NULL key `(k.., d)` of the outer table itself becom
          +es `LAG` (`LEAD`), only when the outer                        
      255 +       query reads every row of that table.                   
      256 +     - A derived table that only filters and projects is merge
          +d into its outer query (as the FROM                           
      257 +       item or an inner join; on the NULL side of a `LEFT JOIN
          +` when it reads one table and outputs                         
      258 +       its columns, its WHERE moving into ON).                
      259 +     - `o LEFT JOIN (SELECT k, AGG … GROUP BY k) t ON t.k = o.
          +c`, read only through its aggregates                          
      260 +       (counts as `COALESCE(t.n, 0)`), → `LEFT JOIN b` grouped
          + by the outer keys; also under one                            
      261 +       outer aggregate. `COUNT(t.x)` over `(SELECT DISTINCT g.
          +., x …) t GROUP BY t.g..` →                                   
      262 +       `COUNT(DISTINCT x)`.                                   
      263 +     - `SELECT DISTINCT … FROM (A UNION ALL B) x …` → the `UNI
          +ON` of the query over each branch.                            
      264 +     - `SELECT [DISTINCT] unnest(ARRAY[a, b])` and `CROSS JOIN
          + LATERAL (VALUES (a), (b)) v(c)` →                            
      265 +       `UNION [ALL]` of one branch per element.               
      266 +     - `AGG(x) OVER ()` in an ungrouped SELECT → the scalar su
          +bquery over the same FROM / WHERE;                            
      267 +       `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY O) <= n` (no partition) in
          + a derived table → `ORDER BY O LIMIT n`                       
      268 +       when the outer order is O itself or does not count.    
      269     - *Row limits and set operations:*
      270       - `OFFSET 0` is dropped, and `LIMIT n` (n ≥ 1) on an ungr
           ouped aggregate (exactly one row).
      271       - `SELECT [DISTINCT] <all columns> FROM (<set operation>)
            t` is the set operation; with
     ...
      314         `COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE c IS NOT NULL)` and `COUNT(*) -
            COUNT(c)` is `COUNT(*) FILTER
      315         (WHERE c IS NULL)`; in an ungrouped SELECT whose aggreg
           ates share one filter, the filter
      316         moves to `WHERE`.
      294 -     - `~* 'lit'`, `lower(x) LIKE` and `ILIKE` are unified.   
      317 +     - `~* 'lit'`, `lower(x) LIKE` and `ILIKE` are unified; `s
          +trpos(s, 'p') > 0` and                                        
      318 +       `position('p' IN s) > 0` are `s LIKE '%p%'` (p without 
          +wildcards); `char_length` is `length`.                        
      319 +     - `= ANY (ARRAY[…])` / `<> ALL (ARRAY[…])` are `IN` / `NO
          +T IN` lists; `(a, b) = (x, y)` is                             
      320 +       `a = x AND b = y` (`<>`: the `OR`); `IS [NOT] DISTINCT 
          +FROM` between never-NULL operands is                          
      321 +       `=` / `<>`; `COALESCE(x, c) = c` is `x IS NULL OR x = c
          +`; a simple `CASE x WHEN v` is a                              
     … 132 more lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I added rules for every group except 10 and 12. False negatives went from 96
  to 11, with no false positives, and the unit tests pass. Nothing is committed.

  accuracy 99.6% (was 96.2%)   precision 100%   recall 99.1% (was 92.0%)   FN 11
    FP 0
  hand-written pairs 977/988 (was 892/988); auto and malformed still 100%

  How I checked soundness:
  - Dataset and stress runs after every batch of rules: the full dataset, every
    breaking mutation of every target (677), and every prediction compared
    against every other target in its schema (55,156 comparisons). The stress
    results stayed exactly at the baseline: 4 matches, all correct ones (an
    unquoted output alias, and predictions that really do equal a neighbouring
    target).
  - Unit tests: about 100 new pairs in tests/test_equivalence.py, each rule
    tested where its condition holds and on a near-miss where it fails.
  - Determinism: output is byte-identical across worker counts.

  What was added, by group

  All of it is in canonical.py (sqleval/canonical.py), plus one serializer
  change.
  - 1. Expressions:
    - = ANY (ARRAY) / <> ALL (ARRAY), row-value =/<>, IS DISTINCT FROM on
      never-NULL operands;
    - COALESCE(x, c) = c, char_length as length, strpos/position as LIKE, simple
      CASE as searched CASE;
    - (SELECT COUNT(*) …) = 0 as NOT EXISTS, a - b > 0 on NUMERIC, numeric
      literal spelling, folding date ± interval;
    - CASE normalization: branch order over numeric ranges, COALESCE(CASE …),
      CASE over every enum label.
  - 2. LATERAL: plain lookups and LEFT lookups become joins, aggregates become
    scalar subqueries, top-1 becomes DISTINCT ON, top-n becomes a ROW_NUMBER
    filter, and the previous/next row becomes LAG/LEAD.
  - 3. Windows:
    - named windows are inlined, LAG(x, 1) is LAG(x), and ranking functions
      ignore their frame;
    - a RANGE frame becomes ROWS when its order keys are unique;
    - ROW_NUMBER() <= n without a partition becomes ORDER BY … LIMIT n, and
      AGG() OVER () becomes a scalar subquery.
  - 4. Aggregate facts: integer bounds on COUNT/SUM, aggregates that can't be
    NULL (for NULLS LAST), SUM/COUNT as AVG, a no-op NULLIF divisor, and
    SUM(CASE … 1 ELSE -1).
  - 5. Anti-joins and semi-joins:
    - HAVING COUNT(b.c) = 0 over a LEFT JOIN becomes NOT EXISTS, and row-valued
      IN/NOT IN become EXISTS;
    - an EXISTS over independent tables is split, x = x is dropped, and a GROUP
      BY that covers a key of every table is dropped;
    - anti-joins against derived tables, and an EXISTS that looks up the outer
      row by its own key, are handled.
  - 6. Derived tables:
    - simple derived tables are merged into the outer query, including on the
      nullable side of a LEFT JOIN;
    - a LEFT JOIN of a grouped derived table is rewritten, and COUNT over a
      DISTINCT derived table becomes COUNT(DISTINCT …);
    - DISTINCT over UNION ALL is distributed over the branches, and scalar
      subqueries nested inside expressions are handled.
  - 7. GROUP BY vs DISTINCT: SELECT e.. GROUP BY e.. (same columns, no
    aggregate) is SELECT DISTINCT e...
  - 8. Enum facts: comparisons use declaration order (status < 'done'), and
    enum::text LIKE 'pattern' becomes the set of matching labels.
  - 9. Unpivots: unnest(ARRAY[…]) and LATERAL (VALUES …) become a UNION.
  - 11. Joins: a RIGHT JOIN followed by more joins becomes a LEFT JOIN.

  Bugs found along the way (fixed)

  - A false positive in existing code: the serializer ignored the WINDOW clause,
    so RANK() OVER w matched no matter how w was defined. Inlining named
    windows fixed it.
  - Two latent naming bugs: the semi-join rewrite dropped a derived table's
    output name, and positional renaming renamed copies instead of the tree
    itself. Neither changed a verdict, but both broke later rules.
  - Three holes in my own new rules, caught in review: LAG ignored the outer
    WHERE, UNION ALL branches with different column names were mishandled, and
    one decorrelation paired tables by position. Each now has a test.

  The 11 still missed

  - Out of scope by agreement (4): the recursive-CTE pairs (3) and WITH TIES
    with a unique key (1).
  - Deliberately left as misses (2), because NUMERIC rounding can make each pair
    really differ: distributing a division, and AVG(x) > k vs SUM(x) > k *
    COUNT(*).
  - Need reasoning beyond a local rewrite (5):
    - a LATERAL top-n with grouping inside it vs ROW_NUMBER/RANK over a
      different join (2);
    - a correlated sum over a date range vs a RANGE INTERVAL window;
    - LEAST/GREATEST pairing;
    - a ratio of table counts that relies on a foreign key.

  Things to know

  - Speed: scoring is about 1.4× slower in parallel (about 1,470 pairs/s,
    against 2,014 before these rules on this machine).
  - Changed rules: two existing rules now behave differently. Derived-table
    outputs are sorted before positional renaming, and aggregate bounds use
    strict lower bounds (COUNT(*) > 3 rather than >= 4), so the existing HAVING
    rewrites still match.
  - README: I updated the rule list, results, soundness notes, limitations and
    performance.

❯ Explain the missing 11

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ All 11 are equivalent pairs that the evaluator scores as non-matches (false
  negatives). In every case both queries parse and canonicalize, but no rewrite
  rule makes their canonical forms identical. They fall into three kinds.

  1. Out of scope: groups 10 and 12 (4 pairs)

  Recursive CTEs written differently (3), campus_017 ×2 and project_tracker_006
  - p00227: the target finds the starting course with a join (JOIN courses c …
    WHERE c.code = 'CS301'). The prediction uses a scalar subquery (course_id =
    (SELECT id FROM courses WHERE code = 'CS301')) and reads the result with IN
    (SELECT cid FROM pre) instead of a join.
  - p00231: the CTE is identical, but the outer query uses EXISTS instead of a
    join. The two are only equal because the CTE uses UNION, so its rows are
    already distinct and the join can't duplicate anything. The evaluator
    doesn't reason about what a recursive CTE outputs.
  - p01059: the target starts from the children of task 100. The prediction
    starts from 100 itself and filters it out at the end. These are equal only
    because task 100 can't reappear as its own descendant without a cycle in the
    data, which is a fact about the data.

  Recursive CTEs are compared almost structurally, because proving two
  recursions equal means reasoning about what the recursion produces, not one
  local rewrite.

  WITH TIES with a unique last sort key (1), p01984
  ORDER BY likes DESC, p.id FETCH FIRST 10 ROWS WITH TIES vs LIMIT 10. Since
  p.id is unique, no two rows can tie on the full sort key, so WITH TIES adds
  nothing. Since the fix in step 1, the evaluator always treats WITH TIES as
  different from LIMIT, which is the safe choice. Matching this pair would need
  a check that the sort keys identify a row.

  2. Left as misses on purpose (2 pairs)

  Both pairs are equal in exact arithmetic but can differ in PostgreSQL, because
  NUMERIC division rounds.

  p01445 sales_snowflake_012: distributing a division
  SUM(Q*P*(1 - D/100)) vs SUM(Q*P) - SUM(Q*P*D/100). PostgreSQL rounds the
  result of a NUMERIC division to a number of decimal places that depends on how
  large the operands are. Rounding inside each row's term is not the same as
  rounding a different expression, so for very large values the last digits can
  differ. The README already lists this case.

  p01522 sales_snowflake_018: AVG(x) > 100 vs SUM(x) > 100 * COUNT(*)
  The second form has no division and is exact. AVG divides, so its result is
  rounded (to at least 16 significant digits). If the true average were
  100.000…0001, with more digits than that, AVG would round to exactly 100 and >
  100 would be false, while the SUM form says true. That needs an enormous
  number of rows to happen, but the evaluator only applies rewrites that hold on
  every valid database, so I left it.

  3. Need more than one local rewrite (5 pairs)

  p01914 / p01915 sales_star_025: LATERAL top-3 with grouping vs ROW_NUMBER /
  RANK
  - The target loops over stores. For each one, a LATERAL subquery groups that
    store's sales by product and keeps the top 3: FROM dim_store s CROSS JOIN
    LATERAL (… GROUP BY p.product_key … LIMIT 3).
  - The predictions put dim_store inside a single grouped derived table (GROUP
    BY s.store_key, s.name, p.product_key, p.name) and filter rn <= 3.

  My top-n rule handles a LATERAL over one table with no grouping. Here the
  subquery groups, and the store table sits outside it in the target but inside
  it in the predictions. Matching them means moving a join into or out of a
  grouped query and proving the groups stay the same. p01915 also uses RANK,
  which equals ROW_NUMBER only because (store, product_key) is unique within
  each window partition.

  p01297 project_tracker_025: RANGE INTERVAL window vs correlated subquery
  SUM(SUM(hours)) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY logged_on RANGE BETWEEN
  INTERVAL '6 days' PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) vs (SELECT SUM(x.hours) … WHERE
  x.user_id = te.user_id AND x.logged_on BETWEEN te.logged_on - 6 AND
  te.logged_on). The window sums the per-day totals of the grouped rows; the
  subquery sums raw entries. Matching them means recognizing a window frame as a
  range query over the underlying table, then turning a sum of per-day sums
  into a plain sum.

  p01944 social_graph_002: LEAST/GREATEST vs an ordered self-join
  - The target keeps each mutual-follow pair once with WHERE follower <
    followee.
  - The prediction keeps both directions, then folds them together with SELECT
    DISTINCT LEAST(a,b), GREATEST(a,b) … WHERE a <> b.

  Proving these equal needs two facts: the join condition is symmetric (if (a,b)
  qualifies then so does (b,a)), and LEAST/GREATEST maps both directions to the
  same row. No rule reasons about symmetry, and it doesn't generalize well.

  p02080 social_graph_013: AVG of per-post counts vs a ratio of table counts
  AVG(cnt) over (SELECT p.id, COUNT(l.userId) … posts LEFT JOIN likes … GROUP BY
  p.id) vs (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM likes)::numeric / NULLIF((SELECT COUNT(*) FROM
  posts), 0). Equal because:
  1. The average of the per-post counts is (total of the counts) ÷ (number of
     posts).
  2. Since likes.postId is a NOT NULL foreign key, every like is counted under
     exactly one post, so the total is just COUNT(*) of likes.
  3. AVG is PostgreSQL's own NUMERIC division, so the two divisions give
     identical digits.
  4. With no posts, both sides are NULL.

  The prediction's other half (p02083, the pre-aggregated LEFT JOIN) is now
  matched. This one needs a sum of grouped counts pushed through the foreign
  key, which is a narrow rule for one pattern.

  If you want to close more

  The cheapest next step is the WITH TIES pair: treat it as LIMIT when the sort
  keys include a unique key of the result. Next would be the two sales_star_025
  pairs, which need the "move a join into a grouped derived table" rule and RANK
  = ROW_NUMBER when the ordering is unique. The two rounding pairs (p01445,
  p01522) should stay misses unless you decide to accept results that agree
  everywhere except in NUMERIC rounding.

❯ Explain the issues with tyring to resolve them

⏺ Every fix here runs into the same constraint. The evaluator only rewrites a
  query when the rewrite gives the same result on every database the schema
  allows, and it applies the same rewrites to both queries. A missed match costs
  one false negative; an unsound rule can quietly create false positives across
  the whole dataset. So the question for each pair is less "can I make it
  match" and more "can I make it match without matching anything that differs".

  Recursive CTEs (p00227, p00231, p01059)

  - Comparing recursions means comparing fixpoints. Two recursive CTEs are equal
    when their recursions converge to the same set of rows. A normal rewrite
    only changes text, but here you have to show the anchor and the recursive
    step produce the same rows at every round. That needs a small proof engine,
    not a pattern.
  - Cycles change everything. With UNION ALL, a cycle in the data makes the
    query run forever. With UNION, the query stops. p01059 is only equal "if
    there's no cycle". The dataset treats a query that never ends as having no
    result to compare, but encoding that assumption into rewrites is a judgment
    call. Make it too broadly and you start matching UNION with UNION ALL where
    duplicates are real.
  - The outer-query part (p00231) looks easy but isn't local. "Join to the CTE =
    semi-join" needs the CTE's rows to be distinct. For a non-recursive derived
    table, the DISTINCT rules already prove that. For a recursive one you need
    to know UNION is the top operator and that no later step adds duplicates.
    That's provable here, but every variation (a UNION ALL inside, extra
    columns, a depth counter) breaks it. A depth counter is the classic trap:
    UNION no longer removes the same row reached by two different paths, because
    the depths differ.
  - Rewriting the anchor (p00227) is tempting but risky inside recursion.
    Turning a scalar subquery into a join is a normal rewrite. Inside a
    recursive CTE, though, rules that move conditions or tables between the
    anchor and the recursive step can change what each round sees, so I'd have
    to verify every existing rule against recursive scopes.

  WITH TIES (p01984)

  - Easy only in the simplest case. It needs proof that no two output rows tie
    on the full ORDER BY key. Here p.id is the GROUP BY key, so each row has a
    distinct id. But the proof has to run after grouping, joins and DISTINCT, on
    an output that may be NULL-extended. Two rows NULL-extended by an outer
    join can both have a NULL "unique" key and tie.
  - The cost of being wrong is a real false positive. WITH TIES returning 12
    rows instead of 10 is a visible difference. I just fixed that false
    positive, so I'd want the uniqueness proof to reuse the existing "output
    determines the source rows" logic rather than a new check.

  The rounding pairs (p01445, p01522)

  - The problem isn't code, it's the definition of "equivalent". Both pairs
    agree in exact arithmetic and can differ in PostgreSQL's NUMERIC rounding,
    which picks its number of decimal places from the operands' size and scale.
    Matching them means accepting "equal up to rounding".
  - Once you allow that, the boundary is hard to draw. If AVG(x) > k can match
    SUM(x) > k*COUNT(*), why not SUM(a)/n + SUM(b)/n vs SUM(a+b)/n, or reordered
    multiplications? Each looks harmless, but together they make the evaluator
    say "match" for queries that print different digits. That contradicts the
    README's statement that results are compared exactly.
  - The honest option is a separate mode. For example, a "value-tolerant" flag
    that allows rewrites which are exact over the rationals. Then the default
    stays exact and the choice is explicit, rather than buried in a rule.

  LATERAL top-n with grouping (p01914, p01915)

  Three separate pieces are needed, and each is risky on its own:
  1. Pushing a join into a grouped query. In the target, dim_store sits outside
     the lateral subquery; in the prediction, it's inside the GROUP BY. Moving a
     table into a grouped query is only safe when the join can't multiply or
     drop rows inside each group, roughly when you join along a key and every
     row has a match. Get that wrong and every aggregate is silently wrong.
  2. Partitioning by the correlation key. The lateral filters by f.store_key =
     s.store_key and groups inside. The ROW_NUMBER form partitions by
     s.store_key over the joined rows. Proving these produce the same ranking
     means showing the per-store groups are identical sets, which depends on
     step 1.
  3. RANK = ROW_NUMBER (p01915). True only when the partition plus order keys
     can't tie. Here product_key is unique within a store only because it's a
     grouping key. That's the same uniqueness problem as WITH TIES, applied
     inside a window partition.

  The top-n rule I added deliberately stops at a single ungrouped table. Each
  extension multiplies the number of shapes it must reject correctly.

  RANGE INTERVAL window vs correlated subquery (p01297)

  - The two sides sum different things. The window sums per-day totals over a
    7-day frame. The subquery sums raw entries in a date range. They're equal
    because summing daily sums equals summing the entries, which needs SUM to be
    decomposable over the groups and the frame to cover exactly whole groups.
  - Frame semantics are subtle. A RANGE with an interval offset measures by the
    value of the order key, not by row count. It also has edge rules: NULL order
    keys are peers of each other, and the result depends on sort direction. The
    subquery's BETWEEN te.logged_on - 6 AND te.logged_on must match those
    exactly, including that date - 6 is a date, not a timestamp.
  - Only some aggregates work. SUM and COUNT decompose like this; AVG doesn't
    (an average of daily averages isn't the average). So the rule would need
    per-aggregate cases, and a mistake there would be a false positive.

  LEAST / GREATEST pairs (p01944)

  - It's a symmetry argument. The join condition f2.follower = f1.followee AND
    f2.followee = f1.follower is symmetric, so each mutual pair appears in both
    directions, and LEAST/GREATEST maps both to one row. Rewrite rules work on
    structure, not on symmetry, so this doesn't fit the canonicalizer.
  - Very specific, so low value. A rule for it would recognize one idiom
    (normalizing unordered pairs) and is easy to get wrong. For example, if
    follower <> followee is missing, self-follows behave differently on each
    side. Its share of what a model might actually produce is tiny.

  Ratio of table counts (p02080)

  - Several facts must line up:
    - every like references exactly one post (NOT NULL FK);
    - the per-post counts sum to the table count;
    - AVG and ::numeric / are the same PostgreSQL function, down to the digits;
    - both sides are NULL when there are no posts.
  - The FK step is the risky one. It's only valid because the foreign key
    guarantees no "orphan" likes and the column is NOT NULL. If either
    constraint is missing (a nullable FK, or a filter on the posts side), the
    two sides differ. The rule would need to check these at exactly the right
    point.
  - One pattern. Like LEAST/GREATEST, this is a narrow pattern for which "sum of
    grouped counts through a foreign key" is the general rule. Building that
    general rule properly is a decent chunk of work for one pair.

  Cross-cutting costs

  - Rule interactions. Most of these need new rules that fire before or after
    existing ones. In this session alone, ordering mattered several times: a
    second alias pass broke decorrelation, and the window normalization had to
    run once, late. Every new rule adds orderings that can break existing
    matches.
  - Testing burden. Each rule needs a "holds" test and a "near-miss" test, plus
    the stress runs. The near-miss is the hard part: you have to find the
    variation that breaks the rule's assumption, and it's easy to miss one.
    That's how the three holes I fixed last time got in.
  - Speed. Each pass over the tree costs time. Scoring is already about 1.4×
    slower than before; proofs like the recursive-CTE or window-frame ones would
    be heavier still.
  - Diminishing returns. Recall is 99.1% with zero false positives. The
    remaining pairs are mostly one-off idioms. Each would take more code than
    all of group 1 combined, for one or two pairs, and adds risk to a metric
    (precision) that matters more.

  My suggestion: if you want to keep going, do WITH TIES (reuse the existing
  uniqueness proof) and the RANK = ROW_NUMBER part of p01915. Both reuse logic
  that already exists. Decide on the rounding pairs as a definition question (an
  exact vs a tolerant mode) rather than in code, and leave the recursive-CTE,
  window-frame, symmetry and FK-ratio cases as documented misses.

  query when the rewrite gives the same result on every database the schema
  allows, and it applies the same rewrites to both queries. A missed match costs
  one false negative; an unsound rule can quietly create false positives across
  the whole dataset. So the question for each pair is less "can I make it
  match" and more "can I make it match without matching anything that differs".

     s.store_key and groups inside. The ROW_NUMBER form partitions by
     s.store_key over the joined rows. Proving these produce the same ranking
     means showing the per-store groups are identical sets, which depends on
     step 1.
  3. RANK = ROW_NUMBER (p01915). True only when the partition plus order keys
     can't tie. Here product_key is unique within a store only because it's a
     grouping key. That's the same uniqueness problem as WITH TIES, applied
     inside a window partition.

  The top-n rule I added deliberately stops at a single ungrouped table. Each
  extension multiplies the number of shapes it must reject correctly.

  RANGE INTERVAL window vs correlated subquery (p01297)

  - The two sides sum different things. The window sums per-day totals over a
    7-day frame. The subquery sums raw entries in a date range. They're equal
    because summing daily sums equals summing the entries, which needs SUM to be
    decomposable over the groups and the frame to cover exactly whole groups.
  - Frame semantics are subtle. A RANGE with an interval offset measures by the
    value of the order key, not by row count. It also has edge rules: NULL order
    keys are peers of each other, and the result depends on sort direction. The
    subquery's BETWEEN te.logged_on - 6 AND te.logged_on must match those
    exactly, including that date - 6 is a date, not a timestamp.
  - Only some aggregates work. SUM and COUNT decompose like this; AVG doesn't
    (an average of daily averages isn't the average). So the rule would need
    per-aggregate cases, and a mistake there would be a false positive.

  LEAST / GREATEST pairs (p01944)

  - It's a symmetry argument. The join condition f2.follower = f1.followee AND
    f2.followee = f1.follower is symmetric, so each mutual pair appears in both
    directions, and LEAST/GREATEST maps both to one row. Rewrite rules work on
    structure, not on symmetry, so this doesn't fit the canonicalizer.
  - Very specific, so low value. A rule for it would recognize one idiom
    (normalizing unordered pairs) and is easy to get wrong. For example, if
    follower <> followee is missing, self-follows behave differently on each
    side. Its share of what a model might actually produce is tiny.

  Ratio of table counts (p02080)

  - Several facts must line up:
    - every like references exactly one post (NOT NULL FK);
    - the per-post counts sum to the table count;
    - AVG and ::numeric / are the same PostgreSQL function, down to the digits;
    - both sides are NULL when there are no posts.
  - The FK step is the risky one. It's only valid because the foreign key
    guarantees no "orphan" likes and the column is NOT NULL. If either
    constraint is missing (a nullable FK, or a filter on the posts side), the
    two sides differ. The rule would need to check these at exactly the right
    point.
  - One pattern. Like LEAST/GREATEST, this is a narrow pattern for which "sum of
    grouped counts through a foreign key" is the general rule. Building that
    general rule properly is a decent chunk of work for one pair.

  Cross-cutting costs

  - Rule interactions. Most of these need new rules that fire before or after
    existing ones. In this session alone, ordering mattered several times: a
    second alias pass broke decorrelation, and the window normalization had to
    run once, late. Every new rule adds orderings that can break existing
    matches.
  - Testing burden. Each rule needs a "holds" test and a "near-miss" test, plus
    the stress runs. The near-miss is the hard part: you have to find the
    variation that breaks the rule's assumption, and it's easy to miss one.
    That's how the three holes I fixed last time got in.
  - Speed. Each pass over the tree costs time. Scoring is already about 1.4×
    slower than before; proofs like the recursive-CTE or window-frame ones would
    be heavier still.
  - Diminishing returns. Recall is 99.1% with zero false positives. The
    remaining pairs are mostly one-off idioms. Each would take more code than
    all of group 1 combined, for one or two pairs, and adds risk to a metric
    (precision) that matters more.

  My suggestion: if you want to keep going, do WITH TIES (reuse the existing
  uniqueness proof) and the RANK = ROW_NUMBER part of p01915. Both reuse logic
  that already exists. Decide on the rounding pairs as a definition question (an
  exact vs a tolerant mode) rather than in code, and leave the recursive-CTE,
  window-frame, symmetry and FK-ratio cases as documented misses.

❯ What other coverage area is our hand tests missing?
