  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Your hand cases are strong on joins, subqueries, NULLs, grouping and LIMIT
  ties, but thin or missing in six areas. I counted SQL features across all 993
  hand cases and read the notes on each case to see which traps they test.
  Counts below are the number of hand cases that use the feature.

  1. Invalid queries are the biggest gap

  All 21 cases marked INVALID test one of two things: identifier case
  ("bookAuthors") or functions from other dialects (nvl, ifnull, datediff).
  There are none for queries that parse but would fail in PostgreSQL:
  - Grouping: a column in SELECT that isn't in GROUP BY.
  - Placement: an aggregate in WHERE, or a window function in WHERE or GROUP BY.
  - Names: a column that is ambiguous across joined tables.
  - Set operations: UNION branches with different column counts or incompatible
    types.
  - Types: comparing text to an integer, or SUM over text.
  - DISTINCT: SELECT DISTINCT … ORDER BY a column that isn't in the select list.

  These matter because of how the gate works. It only runs PostgreSQL's parser,
  and earlier this session a case marked INVALID for a GROUP BY error passed it,
  so I relabeled it N. A prediction like that is scored on its canonical form
  as if it would run. That is a scoring hole as well as missing coverage.

  2. The schemas can't express some features

  The 8 schemas have no boolean, timestamptz, json/jsonb, array or float
  columns. Without schema changes, you can't write cases for:
  - Booleans: WHERE flag vs flag = true vs flag IS TRUE (these differ on NULL),
    bool_and/bool_or/every, IS UNKNOWN.
  - Time zones: AT TIME ZONE, and timestamp vs timestamptz comparisons. No case
    mentions time zones at all.
  - Floats: values that can't be compared exactly, and SUM over floats giving
    different results in a different order.

  3. Aggregates

  - 0 uses: ordered-set aggregates (percentile_cont, percentile_disc, mode()
    WITHIN GROUP), array_agg, and the GROUPING() function (even though ROLLUP
    and CUBE are used).
  - ORDER BY inside an aggregate: 0 uses. string_agg(x, ',' ORDER BY y) is the
    one place where order inside a group changes the result, so it is a good
    case for checking that you don't over-normalize.
  - SUM(DISTINCT …) / AVG(DISTINCT …): 1 use.

  4. Window functions

  - 0 uses: ntile, percent_rank, cume_dist, the GROUPS frame mode and the frame
    EXCLUDE clause.
  - first_value/last_value/nth_value: 0 uses. last_value with the default frame
    is the classic trap: it returns the current row's peer group, not the
    partition's last row.
  - dense_rank: 1 use, vs 7 for rank. The rank / dense_rank / row_number
    confusion is a common model error.

  5. Expressions and functions

  ┌────────────────────────┬──────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Area          │   Uses   │           Missing traps            │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ || / concat()          │ 0        │ 'a' || NULL is NULL, but           │
  │                        │          │ concat('a', NULL) is 'a'           │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ trim, replace,         │ 0–1      │                                    │
  │ substring              │          │                                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SIMILAR TO, regex ~    │ 0–1      │                                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Integer / and %        │ 0        │ 17 notes mention truncation, but   │
  │                        │ explicit │ no %/mod at all                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GREATEST/LEAST         │ 1        │ PostgreSQL ignores NULLs in them;  │
  │                        │          │ many other databases don't         │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CAST(x AS t) spelling  │ 0        │ only :: is used                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ BETWEEN SYMMETRIC      │ 0        │                                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Row constructors (a,   │ 1        │                                    │
  │ b) < (c, d)            │          │                                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ to_char, age()         │ 0–1      │                                    │
  └────────────────────────┴──────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  6. Row sources and clauses

  - 0 uses: generate_series (the usual way to fill gaps in a date series, e.g.
    one row per day), NATURAL JOIN and CTE MATERIALIZED hints.
  - 1–4 uses: VALUES lists (1), OFFSET (1, where pagination off-by-one errors
    are common), USING (4, where USING with an outer join merges the column like
    a COALESCE) and FULL JOIN (3).

  Recommended order

  1. Semantic-error INVALID cases (area 1). Highest value, because they show
     whether the gate gives credit to queries that would fail. If they do, the
     gate needs a check against the schema (unknown, ambiguous and ungrouped
     columns), not just more cases.
  2. Concat and string NULL handling, last_value frame, dense_rank,
     GREATEST/LEAST NULLs, and OFFSET. These are cheap to add and match real
     model mistakes.
  3. Ordered-set aggregates, GROUPING(), generate_series, and aggregate ORDER
     BY. These need new targets, not just new predictions.
  4. Boolean, timestamptz and float columns. These need schema changes and new
     queries, so they're the most work.

  I haven't changed anything. I can add any of these, starting with the
  semantic-error cases and a check of how the gate handles them.

  aggregates.

⏺ PostgreSQL check passes with 0 problems and 0 false positives, but has 46
  false negatives mostly due to missing boolean/timestamptz normalization, plus
  three missed invalid queries involving round() and interval comparisons. I'll
  check the existing interval helpers first so the new rules can reuse them.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Found a serious bug: sqlglot parses INTERVAL '0.5 hours' as 5 hours instead of
  half an hour, causing false positives like x > INTERVAL '0.5 hours' matching
  x > INTERVAL '5 hours'. I'll dig into sqlglot's interval parsing to find where
  the fraction gets dropped.

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Found that the regex drops signs and partially matches strings like '-1 day'
  into '5 hours'. I'll anchor it so it only matches a complete '<n> <unit>'
  string, leaving everything else as an unparsed interval literal.

⏺ Bash(.venv/bin/python -c "
      from sqleval.equivalence import compare…)
  ⎿  Updated sqleval/canonical.py (+4 -0)
      28  
      29  from . import semantic
      30  from .schema import Catalog
      31 +                                                               
      32 +# sqlglot splits INTERVAL '<n> <unit>' with an unanchored finda
         +ll, so '0.5 hours' became '5 hours' and                        
      33 +# '-1 day' lost its sign. Split only a whole '<integer> <unit>'
         + string; anything else stays a literal.                        
      34 +exp.INTERVAL_STRING_RE = re.compile(r"^\s*([0-9]+)\s*([a-zA-Z]+
         +)\s*$")                                                        
      35  from .validate import Invalid
      36  
      37  

⏺ Confirmed the patch fixes both false positives. Next, I'll extend the gate's

⏺ I found four root causes: date(x) miscounting empty args as extra, OR-pairing
  reusing already-matched partners, missing De Morgan rules for pushing NOT
  through OR/AND, and ts::date casts not being rewritten to timestamptz
  session-local ranges.

⏺ Found that PostgreSQL rejects a malformed timestamp literal ('2024-03-01x')
  that the gate currently accepts. I'll fix the gate to reject ISO dates with
  stray trailing letters, and update the mutation to shift ISO date literals by

⏺ I've covered all four areas: the semantic-error gate, the cheap hand cases,
  new targets for the missing features, and a ninth schema with native booleans,
  timestamptz and floats. Precision is still 100% with 0 false positives.
  Recall fell from 99.1% to 98.0% (FN 11 → 30), because the new cases are
  harder. Nothing is committed yet.

  ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │                          │        Before         │          Now          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Schemas / questions      │ 8 / 197               │ 9 / 237               │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Pairs (equivalent / not) │ 2,530 (1,205 / 1,325) │ 3,193 (1,508 / 1,685) │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Hand-written pairs       │ 988                   │ 1,363                 │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Accuracy                 │ 99.6%                 │ 99.1%                 │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ False negatives          │ 11                    │ 30                    │
  └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  PostgreSQL check. I ran a real PostgreSQL 16.2 locally (the pgserver package)
  and checked every label two ways:
  - Validity: every target and prediction goes through EXPLAIN. PostgreSQL now
    rejects exactly the pairs labeled invalid.
  - Results: every equivalent pair returns the same rows as its target on 40
    random databases per schema, each under a different session time zone.

  That check found label errors:
  - Six hand cases that PostgreSQL rejects were labeled as valid
    non-equivalents. One was p00137, which I wrongly downgraded from INVALID
    last session; the gate already rejected it, but the dataset builder wasn't
    using the gate.
  - Two automatic mutations produced invalid SQL: misspelt enum labels, and a
    column swap that left GROUP BY 1 pointing at COUNT(*).

  All are fixed, and the builder now uses the harness's own gate. The check is
  kept as generator/check_postgres.py, an optional generator tool with its own
  requirements file; the harness doesn't depend on it.

  False positives found and fixed. The new cases exposed four real ones, all in
  code that predated this work:
  - sqlglot treated NATURAL JOIN as a cross join.
  - sqlglot parsed INTERVAL '0.5 hours' as five hours and dropped the sign from
    '-1 day'.
  - HAVING c > 1, which PostgreSQL rejects, matched HAVING COUNT(*) > 1, because
    sqlglot quietly expanded the alias.
  - Type errors inside an EXISTS subquery's select list were discarded along
    with that list.

  Gate. A new module, sqleval/semantic.py, rejects queries that parse but that
  PostgreSQL refuses to run: misplaced aliases, misplaced aggregates and window
  functions, type mismatches, wrong column counts, DISTINCT ON order, missing
  LATERAL, and similar. It only rejects what PostgreSQL certainly rejects. On
  over 2,700 queries it agrees with PostgreSQL in both directions, and
  tests/test_gate.py keeps 215 of them as tests.

  New coverage.
  - Hand cases on existing targets: about 100 invalid cases, plus OFFSET,
    DENSE_RANK and NATURAL JOIN cases.
  - 17 new targets on the existing schemas: ordered aggregates,
    percentile_cont/mode, GROUPING(), generate_series gap-filling,
    first_value/last_value, ntile/cume_dist, VALUES, FULL JOIN, regex, || vs
    CONCAT, GREATEST, BETWEEN SYMMETRIC.
  - The rideshare schema: 22 targets on three-valued booleans, time zones,
    intervals and floats.
  - New canonicalization rules for booleans, time zones and intervals, plus
    general rules such as CONCAT → ||, trim/lower order and GREATEST. Each rule
    has a "holds" test and a near-miss test, 375 test cases in all. Scoring is
    about 2–8% slower.

  Still missing:
  - The 30 false negatives: 11 are the ones discussed before. The other 19 are
    one-off idioms, such as SIMILAR TO vs ~, array_to_string(array_agg(…)) and
    conditional aggregation vs FULL JOIN.
  - GROUPS frames and EXCLUDE can't be covered, because sqlglot can't parse
    them.
  - Type checks not added: unknown functions and wrong argument counts. Runtime
    errors aren't caught either.

  The README is updated with the new counts, gate, rules, the PostgreSQL check
  and limitations.

⏺ I'll check rather than assume.

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Correct for the harness. ./score and everything in sqleval/ make no network
  calls, start no database and run no SQL.

  - Imports: apart from the standard library, the only dependencies are sqlglot
    and pglast (requirements.txt). Neither talks to a server.
  - What pglast is: PostgreSQL's parser compiled into a Python library. It turns
    SQL text into a parse tree in-process; it doesn't execute anything.
  - Search: nothing in sqleval/ or score uses psycopg, pgserver, sockets, HTTP,
    subprocess or sqlite. The only concurrency is multiprocessing, for local
    worker processes.

  The one exception is outside the harness. generator/check_postgres.py does
  start a local, embedded PostgreSQL. I used it to check the dataset's labels:
  - Not part of scoring: ./score never imports or calls it.
  - Separate dependencies: its requirements (pgserver, psycopg2-binary) are only
    in generator/requirements-postgres.txt, not in requirements.txt.
  - Local only: it runs a database on this machine, not over the network. The
    only network use was pip downloading those packages, once, into a scratch
    environment.
  - Optional: the README describes it as a dataset check, separate from how
    scoring works.

  If you'd rather the repo contain nothing that runs a database, I can delete
  check_postgres.py and its requirements file and keep the README's audit
  results as a record of what was checked.

⏺ Correct for the harness. ./score and everything in sqleval/ make no network

❯ check_postgres validates that our ground truth queries are real postgres
  queries right? what all does it do?

⏺ Yes, but that's only one of its two checks. It validates every query in
  pairs.jsonl against a real PostgreSQL 16, not just the targets. It also runs
  the pairs on random data to look for labels that are wrong.

  Setup

  - Server: it starts an embedded PostgreSQL in a temp directory using pgserver
    and connects with psycopg2 (check_postgres.py:206-210
    (generator/check_postgres.py#L206-L210)). The server is stopped and the
    directory deleted when it finishes.
  - Schemas: each schema's DDL from dataset/schemas/*.sql is loaded into its own
    PG schema.

  Check 1: validity

  Validity is checked with EXPLAIN, inside a transaction that gets rolled back
  (check_postgres.py:221-238 (generator/check_postgres.py#L221-L238)). EXPLAIN
  parses, analyzes and plans a query without running it. That catches unknown
  tables or columns, type errors, ambiguous references, bad GROUP BY, and so on.
  - Targets: every ground-truth query must be accepted. Any rejection counts as
    a problem.
  - Predictions: PostgreSQL must reject a prediction exactly when it's labeled
    invalid. "Invalid" means the invalid_sql category (hand notes starting with
    INVALID:) or the quoting mutations, where quoting changes identifier case so
    the name no longer resolves. A valid-labeled prediction that PG rejects is
    a problem, and so is an invalid-labeled one that PG accepts. This is what
    audits the semantic-error gate's labels.
  - Skipped: malformed pairs (deliberately unparseable text) are not checked.

  Check 2: results on random databases

  For each schema, it loads a second copy of the DDL with CHECK constraints
  dropped (check_postgres.py:240-265 (generator/check_postgres.py#L240-L265)).
  The harness's definition of equivalence doesn't use CHECK constraints, so data
  that breaks them still counts as a legal database. Then it repeats the
  following 40 times (--seeds):
  1. Fill: it deletes all rows and inserts random ones, parent tables first
     (check_postgres.py:136-185 (generator/check_postgres.py#L136-L185)):
     - PK, UNIQUE, NOT NULL and FK constraints are respected. Nullable columns
       are NULL about 20% of the time, and about 10% of tables are left empty.
     - Values are drawn from literals that appear in the queries, so filters
       actually match rows. Those values are perturbed: numbers ±1, dates ±1 day
       or ±1 month, strings changed in case or given a trailing space.
  2. Time zone: each fill also sets a random session time zone (UTC, New York,
     Kolkata, Chatham, Berlin). This tests the rule that equivalence must hold
     under any time zone.
  3. Compare: it runs every valid target and prediction, each with a 3s timeout.
     Results are compared as multisets; floats are compared as Decimals, and
     arrays become tuples.

  How the comparison results are used:
  - Labeled equivalent: if the results differ on any database, that's a problem.
    This is the main point of the tool: catching pairs labeled equivalent that
    aren't.
  - Labeled not equivalent: these are only counted ("shown different: X/Y"), not
    failed. Some real differences only appear with very specific data that
    random generation rarely produces.

  The script exits 1 if either check finds a problem. The last run found 0.

  What it doesn't do

  - It can't prove equivalence. Random data can only find counterexamples, so an
    "equivalent" label that passes 40 databases is evidence, not proof.
  - No row-order check. Rows that tie under ORDER BY can legitimately come back
    in any order, so ORDER BY differences are left to the unit tests.
  - No runtime errors. EXPLAIN doesn't run the query, so errors like division by
    zero aren't caught as invalid. In check 2, if either query in a pair errors
    at runtime on a given database, that pair is skipped for that database.
  - Not-equivalent labels aren't confirmed. A not-equivalent pair that random
    data never separates doesn't fail.

❯ so its just making sure our equivalent label is correct

⏺ Mostly, but it checks three things:

  1. Targets are real PostgreSQL. Every ground-truth query has to get through
     EXPLAIN against its schema, so a target that references a missing column,
     has a type error or a bad GROUP BY fails here.
  2. Valid and invalid labels are correct. A prediction labeled invalid must be
     rejected by PostgreSQL, and one labeled valid must be accepted. This audits
     the semantic-error gate's cases.
  3. Equivalent labels hold up on data. This is the part you're describing:
     every pair labeled equivalent must return the same rows on all 40 random
     databases.

  The "not equivalent" labels are the one thing it doesn't enforce. It only
  reports how many of those pairs it managed to tell apart, because random data
  can't always hit the difference.

❯ okay as long as we're only using it to ensure our queries and pairs are
  correct is fine. as long as ./score doesnt break the guidelines

⏺ Agreed. check_postgres.py is only used to make sure the dataset's queries and
  labels are correct. ./score never imports it, and nothing it calls touches a
  database or the network.

  ./score sticks to the guidelines. It loads sqleval/, which uses only the
  standard library, sqlglot and pglast. pglast is PostgreSQL's parser compiled
  into the library, so parsing happens in-process. The database packages
  (pgserver, psycopg2) are listed only in generator/requirements-postgres.txt,
  so installing requirements.txt for scoring doesn't pull them in.
